\[ \left(\begin{array}{ccc|c} 1 & 2 & 3 & 0 \end{array}\right) , \]

which is already in reduced row echelon form: its one leading 1 sits in the first column, and there is nothing above or below it to clear. The corresponding equation is

\[ \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} x \\ y \\ z \end{pmatrix} = 0 , \qquad \text{that is} \qquad x + 2y + 3z = 0 . \]

Keep $x$, the unknown carrying the leading 1, on the left and move the rest across:

\[ x = -2y - 3z , \]

with $y$ and $z$ free to be anything in $\mathbb{C}$. So

\[ \begin{aligned} \operatorname{Ker} A &= \{ (x, y, z) \in \mathbb{C}^3 \;:\; x = -2y - 3z \} \\ &= \{ (-2y - 3z,\; y,\; z) \;:\; y, z \in \mathbb{C} \} \\ &= \{ y\,(-2, 1, 0) + z\,(-3, 0, 1) \;:\; y, z \in \mathbb{C} \} \\ &= \operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{ (-2, 1, 0),\, (-3, 0, 1) \} . \end{aligned} \]

Writing those as column vectors,

\[ \operatorname{Ker} A = \operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}} \left\{ \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} -3 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \right\} . \]

Both do satisfy the equation, since $-2 + 2 = 0$ and $-3 + 3 = 0$, and neither is a multiple of the other, so nothing here is wasted.□

Week 5

Example 5.19 (a spanning set for all the products $Ax$) Let $A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 3 & 4 \\ 5 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$. Find a spanning set for \[ \begin{aligned} W &= \left\{ A \begin{pmatrix} x \\ y \end{pmatrix} : x, y \in \mathbb{R} \right\} \\ &= \left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 3 & 4 \\ 5 & 6 \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} x \\ y \end{pmatrix} : x, y \in \mathbb{R} \right\} . \end{aligned} \]
Solution 1

Multiply out, and separate the part with $x$ from the part with $y$:

\[ \begin{aligned} A \begin{pmatrix} x \\ y \end{pmatrix} &= \begin{pmatrix} x + 2y \\ 3x + 4y \\ 5x + 6y \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} x \\ 3x \\ 5x \end{pmatrix} + \begin{pmatrix} 2y \\ 4y \\ 6y \end{pmatrix} \\ &= x \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix} + y \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 4 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix} . \end{aligned} \]

As $x$ and $y$ run through $\mathbb{R}$, these are exactly the linear combinations of the two vectors, so

\[ W = \operatorname{span}\left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 4 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix} \right\} , \]

and these two vectors are a spanning set for $W$.□

Solution 2

Write $\begin{pmatrix} x \\ y \end{pmatrix} = x \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + y \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$, and use that multiplying by $A$ respects addition and multiplication by scalars (Theorem 3.13):

\[ \begin{aligned} A \begin{pmatrix} x \\ y \end{pmatrix} &= A \left( x \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + y \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \right) \\ &= x \, A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + y \, A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \\ &= x \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix} + y \begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 4 \\ 6 \end{pmatrix} , \end{aligned} \]

since $A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = c_1$ is the first column of $A$ and $A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} = c_2$ the second. So $W$ is the span of the columns of $A$:

\[ W = \operatorname{span}\{ c_1, c_2 \} . \]

This is Exercise 3.29 for this $A$: $Ax$ is always a linear combination of the columns of $A$.□

Theorem 5.20 (Theorem 5.22 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$, and let $x_1, x_2, \dots, x_k \in V$. Then $\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\{x_1, x_2, \dots, x_k\}$ is a $\mathbb{K}$-linear subspace of $V$.
Proof

Write $S = \operatorname{span}\{x_1, x_2, \dots, x_k\}$. We check the three conditions of the subspace test, Theorem 5.8.

  1. $\mathbf{0} = 0 x_1 + 0 x_2 + \cdots + 0 x_k$, so $\mathbf{0} \in S$.
  2. Let $v, w \in S$, say $v = \lambda_1 x_1 + \cdots + \lambda_k x_k$ and $w = \mu_1 x_1 + \cdots + \mu_k x_k$ with $\lambda_i, \mu_i \in \mathbb{K}$. Then \[ \begin{aligned} v + w &= (\lambda_1 + \mu_1) x_1 + \cdots \\ &\quad + (\lambda_k + \mu_k) x_k \in S . \end{aligned} \]
  3. Let $v \in S$ be as above, and $\lambda \in \mathbb{K}$. Then \[ \lambda v = (\lambda \lambda_1) x_1 + \cdots + (\lambda \lambda_k) x_k \in S , \] so $S$ is a subspace.□

Sums and intersections of subspaces

Given two subspaces of the same vector space, we can combine them to make a new subspace.

Theorem 5.21 (Theorem 5.27 in the notes) Let $U$ and $V$ be subspaces of a vector space $W$ over $\mathbb{K}$. Then
  1. $U \cap V = \{ w \in W : w \in U$ and $w \in V \}$, the intersection, is a subspace of $W$;
  2. $U + V := \{ u + v : u \in U$ and $v \in V \}$, the sum, is a subspace of $W$.

The notes leave the proof as an exercise, so try it before opening it.

Proof

We check the three conditions of the subspace test, Theorem 5.8, using that $U$ and $V$ satisfy them.

  1. $\mathbf{0} \in U$ and $\mathbf{0} \in V$, so $\mathbf{0} \in U \cap V$. If $w, w' \in U \cap V$, then $w + w' \in U$, as $w, w' \in U$, and $w + w' \in V$, as $w, w' \in V$; so $w + w' \in U \cap V$. In the same way $\lambda w \in U$ and $\lambda w \in V$, so $\lambda w \in U \cap V$.
  2. $\mathbf{0} = \mathbf{0} + \mathbf{0} \in U + V$. Two elements of $U + V$ are $u + v$ and $u' + v'$ with $u, u' \in U$ and $v, v' \in V$, and by the associativity and commutativity of addition, \[ (u + v) + (u' + v') = (u + u') + (v + v') \in U + V , \] since $u + u' \in U$ and $v + v' \in V$. Finally $\lambda (u + v) = \lambda u + \lambda v \in U + V$, since $\lambda u \in U$ and $\lambda v \in V$.□

The union $U \cup V = \{ w \in W : w \in U \text{ or } w \in V \}$ is, in general, not a subspace. In $\mathbb{R}^2$, let $L_1$ and $L_2$ be two different lines through the origin, and take $u \neq \mathbf{0}$ on $L_1$ and $v \neq \mathbf{0}$ on $L_2$. Both lie in $L_1 \cup L_2$, but $u + v$ does not. Two different lines through the origin meet only at $\mathbf{0}$. If $u + v$ were on $L_1$, then $v = (u + v) - u$ would be on $L_1$ as well as on $L_2$, so $v = \mathbf{0}$, which it is not; in the same way, $u + v$ is not on $L_2$. So $L_1 \cup L_2$ is not closed under addition. The picture below shows this. When the two lines are the same, the union is just that line, which is a subspace.

Two lines through the origin: $L_1$ through $\mathbf{0}$ and $u$, and $L_2$ through $\mathbf{0}$ and $v$. Drag near the tip of $u$ or $v$ to turn its line, or focus a dot and use the arrow keys. Put $v$ on the line of $u$, and the two lines become one. Tick the boxes to show $L_1 \cap L_2$, $L_1 \cup L_2$ and $L_1 + L_2$. With the union shown, so is $u + v$, marked green if it lies in the union and red if not. The lines and the shaded plane are cut off only for the picture: they are infinitely big.

Another useful notion is the direct sum, which asks more than the sum of Theorem 5.21.

Definition 5.22 (Direct sum, Definition 5.29 in the notes) A subspace $V \subseteq W$ is the direct sum of two subspaces $V_1, V_2 \subseteq W$, written $V = V_1 \oplus V_2$, if
  1. $V = V_1 + V_2$, and
  2. $V_1 \cap V_2 = \{\mathbf{0}\}$.

In the picture above, while the two lines are different, $L_1 \cap L_2 = \{\mathbf{0}\}$ and $L_1 + L_2 = \mathbb{R}^2$, so $\mathbb{R}^2 = L_1 \oplus L_2$. With $L_1$ the $x$-axis and $L_2$ the $y$-axis this is Example 5.30 in the notes. When they are the same, $L_1 + L_2 = L_1$, but $L_1 \cap L_2 = L_1 \neq \{\mathbf{0}\}$, so that sum is not direct.

If $V$ is the sum of $V_1$ and $V_2$, every element of $V$ is an element of $V_1$ plus an element of $V_2$. If the sum is direct, this can be done in only one way.

Theorem 5.23 (Theorem 5.31 in the notes) Let $V_1$ and $V_2$ be subspaces of a vector space. If $W = V_1 \oplus V_2$, then for every $w \in W$ there are unique $v_1 \in V_1$ and $v_2 \in V_2$ such that \[ w = v_1 + v_2 . \]
Proof

Existence. $W = V_1 + V_2$, so by the definition of the sum there are $v_1 \in V_1$ and $v_2 \in V_2$ with $w = v_1 + v_2$.

Uniqueness. Suppose also $w = v_1' + v_2'$ with $v_1' \in V_1$ and $v_2' \in V_2$. Subtracting one expression for $w$ from the other, \[ \mathbf{0} = (v_1 + v_2) - (v_1' + v_2') = (v_1 - v_1') - (v_2' - v_2) , \] so $v_1 - v_1' = v_2' - v_2$. The left-hand side lies in $V_1$ and the right-hand side in $V_2$, so this one vector lies in $V_1 \cap V_2 = \{\mathbf{0}\}$. Hence $v_1 - v_1' = \mathbf{0}$ and $v_2' - v_2 = \mathbf{0}$, that is, $v_1' = v_1$ and $v_2' = v_2$.□

Question to think about

We understood elementary row operations as multiplication by elementary matrices from the left (the Fact of life, Theorem 3.41 in the notes). How can we understand elementary column operations as multiplication by matrices?

  • What matrices?
  • Multiplication from the right or from the left?
  • Are the solutions of the corresponding equations preserved?
Hint, if you would like one

Can you use $(AB)^t = B^t A^t$, Theorem 3.18, to answer the first and the second? For the third, use an example to show that the answer is no!

Linear independence, bases and dimension

We have met dimension informally, thinking about objects in two or three dimensions. We now begin to make it precise for subspaces, by asking how a subspace can be built from linear combinations of as few vectors as possible. Throughout this chapter $\mathbb{K}$ is a field.

Linear dependence and independence

One way to describe a vector space $V$ is to choose vectors $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k \in V$ that span it, $V = \operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k\}$. To do this efficiently we want as few vectors as possible. If one of them is a linear combination of the others it is redundant, and this leads to the following definition.

Definition 6.1 (Linear dependence and linear independence) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$, and let $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k \in V$.
  1. The vectors $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k$ are linearly dependent if there are $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_k \in \mathbb{K}$, not all $0$, such that \[ \lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k = \mathbf{0} . \]
  2. The vectors $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k$ are linearly independent if they are not linearly dependent, that is, if \[ \lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k = \mathbf{0} \] implies $\lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = \cdots = \lambda_k = 0$. The only way to get $\mathbf{0}$ as a linear combination of them is to take every coefficient $0$.

Part (a) is Definition 6.1 in the notes, and part (b) is Definition 6.4 in the notes. To stress the field we say linearly dependent or independent over $\mathbb{K}$, or $\mathbb{K}$-linearly dependent or independent, because the choice of field can change the answer.

Example 6.2 (one vector, Example 6.2 in the notes) One vector $v$ on its own is linearly dependent exactly when $v = \mathbf{0}$. So a single non-zero vector is linearly independent.
Proof

If $v = \mathbf{0}$, then $1 v = \mathbf{0}$ and the coefficient $1$ is not $0$, so $v$ is linearly dependent. Conversely, suppose $\lambda v = \mathbf{0}$ with $\lambda \neq 0$. Since $\mathbf{0} = 0 v$ by Theorem 5.6 (iii),

\[ \begin{aligned} v &= \lambda^{-1} (\lambda v) = \lambda^{-1} \mathbf{0} = \lambda^{-1} (0 v) \\ &= (\lambda^{-1} \cdot 0)\, v = 0 v = \mathbf{0} , \end{aligned} \]

so $v$ is the zero vector.□

Example 6.3 (two vectors, Example 6.3 in the notes) Two vectors $v_1, v_2$ are linearly dependent if and only if they lie on one line through the origin, that is, one of them is a multiple of the other.
Proof

If $v_1 = \lambda v_2$, then $1 v_1 - \lambda v_2 = \mathbf{0}$, and the coefficient $1$ is not $0$; in the same way if $v_2 = \lambda v_1$. Conversely, suppose $\lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 = \mathbf{0}$ with $\lambda_1, \lambda_2$ not both $0$. If $\lambda_1 \neq 0$, then $v_1 = -\tfrac{\lambda_2}{\lambda_1} v_2$. If $\lambda_1 = 0$, then $\lambda_2 \neq 0$ and $\lambda_2 v_2 = \mathbf{0}$, so $v_2 = \mathbf{0} = 0 v_1$, by Example 6.2 and Theorem 5.6 (iii). Either way one is a multiple of the other.□

Example 6.4 (three vectors) We will see later that three vectors in $\mathbb{R}^3$ are linearly independent if and only if they do not lie in one plane through the origin, that is, if and only if they span $\mathbb{R}^3$. Three vectors that lie in one plane through the origin are linearly dependent: for instance $e_1$, $e_2$ and $e_1 + e_2$, since $1 e_1 + 1 e_2 - (e_1 + e_2) = \mathbf{0}$.
Example 6.5 (the field matters, Example 6.5 in the notes) Are the vectors $\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\begin{pmatrix} i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ in $\mathbb{C}^2$ linearly dependent or linearly independent? It depends on the field.

Choose the correct answers: two of the four are correct.

Correct. Over $\mathbb{C}$ the coefficient $i$ is allowed, and $i \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} - \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ has coefficients $i$ and $-1$, which are not both $0$.
No. Over $\mathbb{C}$ the coefficients may be complex, and $i \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} - \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ has coefficients $i$ and $-1$, not both $0$.
No. Over $\mathbb{R}$ the coefficients must be real, and then $\lambda_1 + i \lambda_2 = 0$ forces $\lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = 0$.
Correct. With $\lambda_1, \lambda_2 \in \mathbb{R}$, the first entry of $\lambda_1 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}$ is $\lambda_1 + i \lambda_2$, and it is $0$ only when $\lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = 0$.
Solution

They are linearly dependent over $\mathbb{C}$, since

\[ i \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} - \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \]

but linearly independent over $\mathbb{R}$. There the coefficients must be real: if $\lambda_1, \lambda_2 \in \mathbb{R}$ and

\[ \begin{aligned} \lambda_1 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} &= \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_1 + i \lambda_2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \\ &= \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \end{aligned} \]

then $\lambda_1 + i \lambda_2 = 0$, and comparing real and imaginary parts gives $\lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = 0$.□

Pick $n$ and click in the picture to add a vector; the shaded region is the span so far, and underneath it says whether the vectors are linearly dependent or linearly independent. With $n = 3$, aim at the ball and drag to turn it. Green means the vector you are about to add is already in the span, so adding it would make the vectors linearly dependent. The square and the cube are only for the picture: a plane and the whole space are infinitely big.

From dependence to a system of equations We can turn the question of whether vectors are linearly dependent or independent into a question about a system of linear equations: whether its solution is unique, or not. Vectors $v_1, \dots, v_k$ give the system $\lambda_1 v_1 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k = \mathbf{0}$ in the unknowns $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k$. It always has the solution $\lambda_1 = \cdots = \lambda_k = 0$, and the vectors are linearly independent exactly when that is the only one.

In each of the next four examples, $A$ is the matrix whose columns are the given vectors, each column in the colour of its vector, and the augmented matrix $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ is already in reduced row echelon form.

Example 6.6 Let us check whether the set \[ \left\{ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} ,\; \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} \right\} \] is linearly independent or not. Consider the vector equation, and the matrix $A$:
\[ \lambda_1 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_2 \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \]
\[ A = \begin{pmatrix} \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{1} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{1} \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} \end{pmatrix} . \]

The vector equation is $A x = \mathbf{0}$, with $x = (\lambda_1, \lambda_2)$, and $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ is

\[ \left(\begin{array}{cc|c} \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{1} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & 0 \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{1} & 0 \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & 0 \end{array}\right) . \]

$2$ leading 1s, $2$ unknowns: the solution is unique, so the set is linearly independent.

Also note that, by Theorem 3.13,

\[ \begin{aligned} A \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_1 \\ \lambda_2 \end{pmatrix} &= A \left( \lambda_1 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \right) \\ &= \lambda_1 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \\ &= \lambda_1 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_2 \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \end{aligned} \]

$\lambda_1$ times the first column of $A$ plus $\lambda_2$ times the second. That is why the vector equation is $A x = \mathbf{0}$. Check by hand that

\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]
\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]

the first and second columns of $A$.

Example 6.7 Let us check whether the set \[ \left\{ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} ,\; \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} ,\; \textcolor{#1a7f37}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}} \right\} \] is linearly independent or not. Consider the vector equation, and the matrix $A$:
\[ \lambda_1 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_2 \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_3 \textcolor{#1a7f37}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \]
\[ A = \begin{pmatrix} \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{1} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{0} \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{1} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{0} \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{1} \end{pmatrix} . \]

The vector equation is $A x = \mathbf{0}$, with $x = (\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3)$, and $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ is

\[ \left(\begin{array}{ccc|c} \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{1} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{0} & 0 \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{1} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{0} & 0 \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{1} & 0 \end{array}\right) . \]

$3$ leading 1s, $3$ unknowns: the solution is unique, so the set is linearly independent.

Also note that, by Theorem 3.13,

\[ \begin{aligned} & A \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_1 \\ \lambda_2 \\ \lambda_3 \end{pmatrix} \\ & = A \left( \lambda_1 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_3 \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \right) \\ & = \lambda_1 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_3 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \\ & = \lambda_1 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_2 \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_3 \textcolor{#1a7f37}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}} , \end{aligned} \]

$\lambda_1$ times the first column of $A$, plus $\lambda_2$ times the second, plus $\lambda_3$ times the third. That is why the vector equation is $A x = \mathbf{0}$. Check by hand that

\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]
\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]
\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#1a7f37}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}} , \]

the first, second and third columns of $A$.

Example 6.8 Let us check whether the set \[ \left\{ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} ,\; \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} \right\} \] is linearly independent or not. It is easy to see that
\[ 2 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} = \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]
\[ \text{or} \quad 2 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} - \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \]
so $\lambda_1 = 2$, $\lambda_2 = -1$ is already a non-zero solution of the vector equation below, and the set is linearly dependent. But let us explore this more systematically. Consider the vector equation, and the matrix $A$:
\[ \lambda_1 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_2 \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \]
\[ A = \begin{pmatrix} \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{1} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{2} \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} \end{pmatrix} . \]

The vector equation is $A x = \mathbf{0}$, with $x = (\lambda_1, \lambda_2)$, and $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ is

\[ \left(\begin{array}{cc|c} \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{1} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{2} & 0 \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & 0 \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & 0 \end{array}\right) . \]

$1$ leading 1, $2$ unknowns: $\lambda_2$ is free, so the solution is not unique, and the set is linearly dependent.

Also note that, by Theorem 3.13,

\[ \begin{aligned} A \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_1 \\ \lambda_2 \end{pmatrix} &= A \left( \lambda_1 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \right) \\ &= \lambda_1 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \\ &= \lambda_1 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_2 \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \end{aligned} \]

$\lambda_1$ times the first column of $A$ plus $\lambda_2$ times the second. That is why the vector equation is $A x = \mathbf{0}$. Check by hand that

\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]
\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]

the first and second columns of $A$.

Example 6.9 Let us check whether the set \[ \left\{ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{e_1} ,\; \textcolor{#eb811b}{e_2} ,\; \textcolor{#1a7f37}{e_3} ,\; \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{2e_1 + 3e_3} \right\} \] is linearly independent or not. Consider the vector equation, and the matrix $A$:
\[ \begin{aligned} & \lambda_1 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_2 \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_3 \textcolor{#1a7f37}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}} \\ & \quad {} + \lambda_4 \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \end{aligned} \]
\[ A = \begin{pmatrix} \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{1} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{0} & \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{2} \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{1} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{0} & \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{0} \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{1} & \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{3} \end{pmatrix} . \]

The vector equation is $A x = \mathbf{0}$, with $x = (\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3, \lambda_4)$, and $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ is

\[ \left(\begin{array}{cccc|c} \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{1} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{0} & \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{2} & 0 \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{1} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{0} & \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{0} & 0 \\ \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{0} & \textcolor{#eb811b}{0} & \textcolor{#1a7f37}{1} & \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{3} & 0 \end{array}\right) . \]

$3$ leading 1s, $4$ unknowns: $\lambda_4$ is free, so the solution is not unique, and the set is linearly dependent.

Also note that, by Theorem 3.13,

\[ \begin{aligned} & A \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_1 \\ \lambda_2 \\ \lambda_3 \\ \lambda_4 \end{pmatrix} \\ & = A \left( \lambda_1 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \cdots + \lambda_4 \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \right) \\ & = \lambda_1 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \cdots + \lambda_4 \, A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \\ & = \lambda_1 \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_2 \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} \\ & \quad {} + \lambda_3 \textcolor{#1a7f37}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}} + \lambda_4 \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}} , \end{aligned} \]

$\lambda_1$ times the first column of $A$, plus $\lambda_2$ times the second, and so on. That is why the vector equation is $A x = \mathbf{0}$. Check by hand that

\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#2a6fb5}{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]
\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#eb811b}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} , \]
\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#1a7f37}{\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}} , \]
\[ A \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} = \textcolor{#7a3fa0}{\begin{pmatrix} 2 \\ 0 \\ 3 \end{pmatrix}} , \]

the first, second, third and fourth columns of $A$.

Linear independence by row reduction So $v_1, \dots, v_k$ are linearly independent if and only if $A x = \mathbf{0}$ has a unique solution, if and only if the reduced row echelon form of the augmented matrix $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ has as many leading 1s as there are unknowns.

Remember that each unknown with a leading 1 can be written in terms of the others, which are free. For the solution to be unique, there must be no free unknowns.

Theorem 6.10 (Theorem 6.8 in the notes) Let $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k \in \mathbb{K}^n$, and let $A \in M_{n,k}(\mathbb{K})$ be the matrix whose columns are $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k$. Then $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb{K}$ if $S(A, \mathbf{0}) = \{\mathbf{0}\}$, and linearly dependent otherwise.
Proof

For $x = (\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_k)$, Exercise 3.29 gives

\[ A x = \lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k . \]

So $Ax = \mathbf{0}$ has a solution $x \neq \mathbf{0}$ exactly when some linear combination of $v_1, \dots, v_k$ with coefficients not all $0$ is $\mathbf{0}$, that is, exactly when the vectors are linearly dependent. Since $x = \mathbf{0}$ is always a solution, $S(A, \mathbf{0}) = \{\mathbf{0}\}$ exactly when they are linearly independent.□

Corollary 6.11 (Corollary 6.11 in the notes) Let $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k \in \mathbb{K}^n$. If $k > n$, then $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k$ are linearly dependent over $\mathbb{K}$. Equivalently, if $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb{K}$, then $k \leq n$. So a set of linearly independent vectors in $\mathbb{K}^n$ has at most $n$ elements.
Proof

We prove the first form; the second is its contrapositive. Each $v_i$ is in $\mathbb{K}^n$, so the matrix with columns $v_1, \dots, v_k$ is

\[ A = \bigl( v_1 \mid v_2 \mid \cdots \mid v_k \bigr)_{n \times k} . \]

Now let $k > n$, and consider the equation $A x = \mathbf{0}$, that is,

\[ \lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k = \mathbf{0} . \]

It has $k$ unknowns, $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k$. In a row echelon form of $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ each leading 1 sits in a row of its own, and there are $n$ rows, so there are at most $n$ leading 1s. Since $k > n$, there are more unknowns than unknowns with a leading 1, so at least one unknown is free. The last column of $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ is zero, so it has no leading 1, and by Theorem 3.27 (iii) the equation has infinitely many solutions. So $S(A, \mathbf{0}) \neq \{\mathbf{0}\}$, and $v_1, \dots, v_k$ are linearly dependent by Theorem 6.10.□

Example 6.12 (too many vectors) Any $4$, $5$ or $6$ vectors in $\mathbb{R}^3$ are linearly dependent, by Corollary 6.11: linearly independent vectors in $\mathbb{R}^3$ number at most $3$. In terms of equations, $k$ vectors give an augmented matrix $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ with $3$ rows, so at most $3$ leading 1s; with $k = 4$, $5$ or $6$ unknowns, at least $1$, $2$ or $3$ of them are free, and the solution is not unique. Example 6.9 is a case with $k = 4$.
Example 6.13 (square matrices) If we have three vectors $v_1, v_2, v_3$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$, it is very easy to say whether they are linearly independent or not. Why? Because the parallelepiped they span has volume zero exactly when they are linearly dependent, that is, when it is flat and lies in a plane through the origin. That volume is $|\det(v_1 \mid v_2 \mid v_3)|$, where $(v_1 \mid v_2 \mid v_3)$ is the matrix with $v_1, v_2, v_3$ as its columns: see the three-dimensional picture in Chapter 4. Equivalently, \[ \begin{gathered} v_1, v_2, v_3 \text{ are linearly dependent} \\ \iff \det(v_1 \mid v_2 \mid v_3) = 0 . \end{gathered} \] Since $\det A = \det A^t$ (Theorem 4.13), we can say the same thing with $v_1, v_2, v_3$ as the rows of the matrix. This needs a square matrix: $k \neq n$ vectors in $\mathbb{R}^n$ give an $n \times k$ matrix, which has no determinant, and then we row reduce, as in Examples 6.6 to 6.9. More generally, let $v_1, \dots, v_n \in \mathbb{R}^n$, and let $P$ be the $n$-dimensional parallelepiped they span. Then \[ \begin{aligned} & \operatorname{vol}(P) \neq 0 \\ & \iff \text{they do not lie in one } (n-1)\text{-} \\ & \qquad \text{dimensional plane through } \mathbf{0} \\ & \iff \text{they are linearly independent,} \end{aligned} \] but $\operatorname{vol}(P) = |\det(v_1 \mid \cdots \mid v_n)|$, so also \[ \operatorname{vol}(P) \neq 0 \iff \det(v_1 \mid \cdots \mid v_n) \neq 0 . \] Together: $v_1, \dots, v_n$ are linearly independent if and only if $\det(v_1 \mid \cdots \mid v_n) \neq 0$. The next theorem proves this, and more.
Theorem 6.14 (Theorem 6.12 in the notes) Let $A \in M_n(\mathbb{K})$. The following are equivalent.
  1. $\det A \neq 0$.
  2. $A$ is invertible.
  3. The equation $A x = \mathbf{0}$ has a unique solution, $x = \mathbf{0}$.
  4. The columns of $A$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb{K}$.
  5. The rows of $A$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb{K}$.

Exercise 4.14 showed that (a) and (b) are equivalent, and that they hold exactly when $A x = b$ has exactly one solution for every $b$; its solution needs only $b = \mathbf{0}$, which is (c). Theorem 6.12 in the notes lists (a), (b), (d) and (e). Here, as in the notes, $\mathbb{K}$ is $\mathbb{R}$ or $\mathbb{C}$. Chapters 3 and 4 worked with real matrices, and their proofs go through unchanged for complex ones.

Proof

We go round the loop (a) $\Rightarrow$ (d) $\Rightarrow$ (c) $\Rightarrow$ (b) $\Rightarrow$ (a), the notes' loop with (c) added, and then show that (d) and (e) are equivalent.

(a) implies (d). We prove the contrapositive. Suppose the columns $a_1, \dots, a_n$ of $A$ are linearly dependent, so that $\lambda_1 a_1 + \cdots + \lambda_n a_n = \mathbf{0}$ with some $\lambda_i \neq 0$. These columns are the rows of $A^t$. In $A^t$, add $\lambda_j / \lambda_i$ times row $j$ to row $i$, for every $j \neq i$. Row $i$ becomes

\[ \begin{aligned} a_i + \sum_{j \neq i} \frac{\lambda_j}{\lambda_i} a_j &= \frac{1}{\lambda_i} \sum_{j=1}^{n} \lambda_j a_j \\ &= \mathbf{0} . \end{aligned} \]

By Lemma 4.6 (ii) these operations do not change the determinant, and the matrix they give has a zero row, so $\det A^t = 0$ by Lemma 4.5. Hence $\det A = \det A^t = 0$, by Theorem 4.13.

(d) implies (c). If the columns of $A$ are linearly independent, then $S(A, \mathbf{0}) = \{\mathbf{0}\}$ by Theorem 6.10: the system $A x = \mathbf{0}$ has exactly one solution, $x = \mathbf{0}$.

(c) implies (b). If $A x = \mathbf{0}$ has exactly one solution, then by Theorem 3.27 (ii) every column of a row echelon form of $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$ except the last has a leading 1. So the reduced row echelon form of $A$ is a square matrix with a leading 1 in every column, which is $I_n$. Row reduction takes $A$ to $I_n$, and $A$ is invertible by Theorem 3.36.

(b) implies (a). This is Exercise 4.10 (a): taking determinants of $A A^{-1} = I_n$, Theorem 4.9 gives $\det(A) \det(A^{-1}) = 1$, so $\det A \neq 0$.

(d) and (e) are equivalent. The rows of $A$ are the columns of $A^t$. By the loop, applied to $A$ and to $A^t$, (d) holds exactly when $\det A \neq 0$, and (e) holds exactly when $\det A^t \neq 0$. These are the same condition, since $\det A^t = \det A$ by Theorem 4.13.□

Bases

We have built spaces from sets of vectors by taking their span, and linear independence now gives us a way to avoid any redundancy in our choice of vectors in a spanning set. First, a connection between linear dependence and spans.

Lemma 6.15 (Linear dependence and span, Lemma 6.13 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$ and $S \subseteq V$. Then $S$ is linearly dependent if and only if there exists a $v \in S$ such that \[ \operatorname{span} S = \operatorname{span}(S \setminus \{v\}) . \]

In other words, $S$ is linearly dependent exactly when one of its vectors is a linear combination of the others, that is, lies in the span of the others: removing it does not change the span. The dependence picture above shows it: there, a vector already in the span of those before it makes the vectors dependent, and adding it leaves the shaded span as it was.

Proof

If $\operatorname{span} S = \operatorname{span}(S \setminus \{v\})$, then $v \in \operatorname{span}(S \setminus \{v\})$, meaning that $v$ is a linear combination $v = \gamma_1 w_1 + \cdots + \gamma_m w_m$ of some $w_1, \dots, w_m \in S \setminus \{v\}$. Then

\[ \gamma_1 w_1 + \cdots + \gamma_m w_m - v = \mathbf{0} \]

is a linear combination of vectors of $S$ equal to $\mathbf{0}$ in which the coefficient of $v$ is $-1 \neq 0$, and so $S$ is linearly dependent.

Now assume $S$ is linearly dependent: $\lambda_1 v_1 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k = \mathbf{0}$ for some distinct $v_1, \dots, v_k \in S$ with some $\lambda_j \neq 0$. Dividing by $\lambda_j$ and moving the other terms across, the element $v = v_j \in S$ can be written as a linear combination

\[ v = \sum_{i \neq j} \mu_i v_i , \qquad \mu_i = -\frac{\lambda_i}{\lambda_j} , \]

of the $v_i \in S \setminus \{v\}$. Every linear combination of elements of $S \setminus \{v\}$ is also a linear combination of elements of $S$, so $\operatorname{span}(S \setminus \{v\}) \subseteq \operatorname{span} S$. Now assume $y \in \operatorname{span} S$. Then $y$ can be written as a linear combination of elements of $S$. If $v$ is not contained in this linear combination, then $y \in \operatorname{span}(S \setminus \{v\})$. If $v$ is contained in it, then, with $w_1, \dots, w_m \in S \setminus \{v\}$,

\[ \begin{aligned} y &= \alpha v + \beta_1 w_1 + \cdots + \beta_m w_m \\ &= \alpha \sum_{i \neq j} \mu_i v_i + \beta_1 w_1 + \cdots + \beta_m w_m \\ &= \sum_{i \neq j} (\alpha \mu_i) v_i + \beta_1 w_1 + \cdots + \beta_m w_m , \end{aligned} \]

and both sums in the last line lie in $\operatorname{span}(S \setminus \{v\})$. Hence $y \in \operatorname{span}(S \setminus \{v\})$, and $\operatorname{span} S = \operatorname{span}(S \setminus \{v\})$.□

A random set of vectors, with its span shaded. Point at a vector to highlight it: green if it lies in the span of the others, so that removing it leaves the span as it is (Lemma 6.15), red if removing it makes the span smaller. Click it to remove it. With $n = 3$, drag anywhere else to turn the picture.

Linearly independent vectors, with their span shaded. Click to add a vector. One outside the span keeps the set linearly independent (Exercise 6.35) and makes the span bigger, so it is added, drawn dark beside the orange ones you started with; one inside the span would make the set linearly dependent, so it is not. Pointing inside the span turns it green. Keep adding until the span is the whole space: the set is then a basis that contains the vectors you started with (Theorem 6.33). With $n = 3$, drag to turn the picture.

If a collection of vectors spans a subspace and is linearly independent, it deserves a special name.

Definition 6.16 (Basis, Definition 6.14 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$. A basis of $V$ is a set of vectors $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k \in V$ such that
  1. $\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{K}}\{v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k\} = V$, and
  2. the vectors $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb{K}$.
The plural of basis is bases, and we will often write $B = \{v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k\}$ for a basis.

So a basis of $V$ is a set of vectors in $V$ that spans the whole of $V$ with nothing to spare: since it is linearly independent, Lemma 6.15 says that removing any one of its vectors makes the span smaller. In the picture above, the vectors are a basis of their span exactly when every one of them turns red.

Example 6.17 (true or false) A spanning set of a subspace is always a subset of that subspace. True or false?
Answer

True. Let $W$ be a subspace, and let $\{v_1, \dots, v_k\}$ be a spanning set for $W$, so that $\operatorname{span}\{v_1, \dots, v_k\} = W$ (Definition 5.12 (c)). Each $v_i$ is the linear combination of $v_1, \dots, v_k$ with coefficient $1$ for $v_i$ and $0$ for the others, so $v_i \in \operatorname{span}\{v_1, \dots, v_k\} = W$. Hence $\{v_1, \dots, v_k\} \subseteq W$.□

Definition 6.18 (Standard basis, Definition 6.16 in the notes) The set $e_1 = (1, 0, 0, \dots, 0)$, $e_2 = (0, 1, 0, \dots, 0)$, $e_3 = (0, 0, 1, \dots, 0)$, $\dots$, $e_n = (0, 0, 0, \dots, 1) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ is called the standard basis for $\mathbb{R}^n$ over $\mathbb{R}$.

It is a basis, which is Example 6.15 in the notes: every $v = (x_1, x_2, \dots, x_n) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ is $x_1 e_1 + x_2 e_2 + \cdots + x_n e_n$, so the $e_i$ span $\mathbb{R}^n$; and the matrix with columns $e_1, \dots, e_n$ is $I_n$, so they are linearly independent by Theorem 6.10.

Exercise 6.19 (after Definition 6.16 in the notes) Is $e_1, \dots, e_n \in \mathbb{C}^n$ a basis for $\mathbb{C}^n$ over $\mathbb{C}$? What about over $\mathbb{R}$?
Solution

Over $\mathbb{C}$, yes, by the same argument as for $\mathbb{R}^n$: every $v = (z_1, \dots, z_n) \in \mathbb{C}^n$ is $z_1 e_1 + \cdots + z_n e_n$ with every $z_i \in \mathbb{C}$, and the matrix with columns $e_1, \dots, e_n$ is $I_n$. So we may call it the standard basis for $\mathbb{C}^n$ over $\mathbb{C}$ too. For $\mathbb{C}^2$ over $\mathbb{C}$ it is the two vectors $e_1 = (1, 0)$ and $e_2 = (0, 1)$.

Over $\mathbb{R}$, no: it is not a spanning set, since no $\mathbb{R}$-linear combination of $e_1, \dots, e_n$ gives $(i, 0, \dots, 0)$, for example. A basis for $\mathbb{C}^n$ over $\mathbb{R}$ is $e_1, \dots, e_n, i e_1, \dots, i e_n$. For $\mathbb{C}^2$ over $\mathbb{R}$ it is the four vectors $e_1 = (1, 0)$, $e_2 = (0, 1)$, $i e_1 = (i, 0)$ and $i e_2 = (0, i)$: every $(a_1 + i b_1, a_2 + i b_2)$ with $a_1, a_2, b_1, b_2 \in \mathbb{R}$ is $a_1 e_1 + a_2 e_2 + b_1 (i e_1) + b_2 (i e_2)$.□

Definition 6.20 (Basis of $\{\mathbf{0}\}$, Example 6.17 in the notes) We define the basis of $\{\mathbf{0}\}$ to be $\emptyset$, the empty set.

This is the natural choice: the span of the empty set is $\{\mathbf{0}\}$, since with nothing to combine $\mathbf{0}$ is all we can make, and with no vectors there is no linear dependence among them.

The definition of a basis means that every vector can be written as a linear combination of the basis vectors in exactly one way.

Theorem 6.21 (Theorem 6.18 in the notes) Let $W$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$, let $V \subseteq W$ be a subspace, and let $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k \in V$ be a basis of $V$. Then for every $v \in V$ there are unique scalars $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \dots, \lambda_k \in \mathbb{K}$ such that \[ v = \lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k . \]
Proof

Since $v_1, \dots, v_k$ span $V$, for every $v \in V$ there are scalars $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k \in \mathbb{K}$ with

\[ v = \lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k . \]

We have to show that these scalars are unique. So suppose that there are others, $\mu_1, \dots, \mu_k$, with

\[ v = \mu_1 v_1 + \mu_2 v_2 + \cdots + \mu_k v_k . \]

Subtracting the first from the second gives

\[ \begin{aligned} \mathbf{0} &= (\mu_1 - \lambda_1) v_1 + (\mu_2 - \lambda_2) v_2 \\ &\quad + \cdots + (\mu_k - \lambda_k) v_k , \end{aligned} \]

and since $v_1, \dots, v_k$ are linearly independent, $\mu_1 - \lambda_1 = \mu_2 - \lambda_2 = \cdots = \mu_k - \lambda_k = 0$, that is, $\mu_i = \lambda_i$ for every $i$.□

Example 6.22 (before Example 6.20 in the notes) Is $B = \{e_1, e_2, e_3\}$ a basis for $V = \{(x, 2x, z) : x, z \in \mathbb{R}\} \subseteq \mathbb{R}^3$?
Solution

No. A basis of $V$ is a set of vectors in $V$ (Definition 6.16), but $e_1 = (1, 0, 0)$ is not in $V$: a vector $(x, 2x, z)$ with $x = 1$ has second component $2$, not $0$. Nor is $e_2 = (0, 1, 0)$, whose first component $0$ would need a second component $0$. This is Example 6.17 at work: a spanning set for $V$ lies inside $V$, and $e_1$ and $e_2$ do not. Indeed $e_1, e_2, e_3$ span all of $\mathbb{R}^3$, which is more than $V$.

A basis of $V$ is $\{(1, 2, 0), (0, 0, 1)\}$. Every vector of $V$ is a combination of the two, $(x, 2x, z) = x (1, 2, 0) + z (0, 0, 1)$ for all $x$ and $z$, and the two are linearly independent, since neither is a multiple of the other (Example 6.3).□

Exercise 6.23 (a basis of a solution set) Find a basis for the solution set of the system \[ \begin{aligned} x + 2y - z &= 0 , \\ 2x + y + z &= 0 . \end{aligned} \]
Solution

The solution set is $S(A, \mathbf{0})$ for $A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & -1 \\ 2 & 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$, which is a subspace of $\mathbb{R}^3$ by Theorem 5.11, so it makes sense to ask for a basis. Row reduce $(A \mid \mathbf{0})$:

\[ \begin{aligned} & \left(\begin{array}{ccc|c} 1 & 2 & -1 & 0 \\ 2 & 1 & 1 & 0 \end{array}\right) \\ \longrightarrow\; & \left(\begin{array}{ccc|c} 1 & 2 & -1 & 0 \\ 0 & -3 & 3 & 0 \end{array}\right) \\ \longrightarrow\; & \left(\begin{array}{ccc|c} 1 & 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & -1 & 0 \end{array}\right) , \end{aligned} \]

by $R_2 \to R_2 - 2R_1$, then $R_2 \to -\tfrac13 R_2$ and $R_1 \to R_1 - 2R_2$. So $z$ is free, and $x = -z$, $y = z$: the solutions are

\[ \begin{pmatrix} x \\ y \\ z \end{pmatrix} = z \begin{pmatrix} -1 \\ 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} , \qquad z \in \mathbb{R} . \]

So the solution set is $\operatorname{span}\{(-1, 1, 1)\}$, and $\{(-1, 1, 1)\}$ is a basis of it: it spans, and a single non-zero vector is linearly independent (Example 6.2).□

Given a basis $v_1, \dots, v_k$ of $V$, it is not always straightforward to write a vector $v$ in terms of it, that is, to find the numbers $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k \in \mathbb{K}$. In general this leads to a system of linear equations for $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k$.

Example 6.24 (Example 6.20 in the notes) Let $v_1 = (1, 2, 3)$, $v_2 = (-1, 2, 1)$ and $v_3 = (0, 0, 1)$. Do they form a basis of $\mathbb{R}^3$? If so, write every $w = (x, y, z) \in \mathbb{R}^3$ in terms of them.
Solution 1

We have to find $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3$ with $\lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 + \lambda_3 v_3 = w$. Written out, this is a system of three linear equations in three unknowns, with augmented matrix

\[ \left(\begin{array}{c|c} A & w \end{array}\right) = \left(\begin{array}{ccc|c} 1 & -1 & 0 & x \\ 2 & 2 & 0 & y \\ 3 & 1 & 1 & z \end{array}\right) . \]

By $R_2 \to R_2 - 2R_1$, $R_3 \to R_3 - 3R_1$, $R_3 \to R_3 - R_2$ and $R_2 \to \tfrac14 R_2$ we reach the row echelon form

\[ \left(\begin{array}{ccc|c} 1 & -1 & 0 & x \\ 0 & 1 & 0 & \tfrac{y}{4} - \tfrac{x}{2} \\ 0 & 0 & 1 & z - y - x \end{array}\right) . \]

Every column of $A$ has a leading 1, so with $w = \mathbf{0}$ the only solution is $\lambda_1 = \lambda_2 = \lambda_3 = 0$, and the vectors are linearly independent. For any $w$, back-substitution gives

\[ \lambda_3 = z - y - x , \]
\[ \lambda_2 = \frac{y}{4} - \frac{x}{2} , \]
\[ \lambda_1 = \frac{y}{4} + \frac{x}{2} , \]

so they also span $\mathbb{R}^3$. Therefore they form a basis, and

\[ \begin{aligned} w &= \Bigl( \frac{y}{4} + \frac{x}{2} \Bigr) v_1 + \Bigl( \frac{y}{4} - \frac{x}{2} \Bigr) v_2 \\ &\quad + (z - y - x) v_3 . \end{aligned} \]

It is worth checking this for one $w$, say $w = (2, 4, 1)$.□

Solution 2, using the next lemma

By Lemma 6.26, three vectors in $\mathbb{R}^3$ form a basis exactly when the determinant of the matrix with them as columns is not $0$. Expanding by column 3 (Theorem 4.11), only the entry $1$ in the corner is not zero, so

\[ \begin{aligned} & \det(v_1 \mid v_2 \mid v_3) \\ &= \begin{vmatrix} 1 & -1 & 0 \\ 2 & 2 & 0 \\ 3 & 1 & 1 \end{vmatrix} \\ &= (-1)^{3+3} \cdot 1 \cdot \begin{vmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ 2 & 2 \end{vmatrix} \\ &= 2 + 2 = 4 \neq 0 . \end{aligned} \]

So $v_1, v_2, v_3$ form a basis of $\mathbb{R}^3$. This settles the first question in one line; writing $w$ in terms of the basis still takes the row reduction of Solution 1, or $A^{-1}$.□

Example 6.25 (three vectors and a determinant) Let us check whether \[ v_1 = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} , \quad v_2 = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} , \quad v_3 = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \] form a basis of $\mathbb{R}^3$. Let \[ A = (v_1 \mid v_2 \mid v_3) = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix} . \] Expanding by row 1 (Theorem 4.11), \[ \begin{aligned} \det A &= 1 \cdot (1 \cdot 0 - 1 \cdot 1) - 0 \\ &\quad + 1 \cdot (0 \cdot 1 - 1 \cdot 1) = -2 \neq 0 . \end{aligned} \]

By Theorem 6.14, (a) implies (d): the columns $v_1, v_2, v_3$ are linearly independent. And (a) implies (b): $A$ is invertible, so for every $w \in \mathbb{R}^3$ the equation $\lambda_1 v_1 + \lambda_2 v_2 + \lambda_3 v_3 = w$, which is $A \lambda = w$ by Exercise 3.29, has the solution $\lambda = A^{-1} w$ (Lemma 3.39). So $v_1, v_2, v_3$ also span $\mathbb{R}^3$, and they form a basis. Had the determinant been $0$, the vectors would have been linearly dependent, by Theorem 6.14 again, and not a basis.

Lemma 6.26 (Determinant test for a basis) Let $\mathbb{K}$ be $\mathbb{R}$ or $\mathbb{C}$, and let $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_n \in \mathbb{K}^n$, with $\mathbb{K}^n$ a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$. Then $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_n$ form a basis of $\mathbb{K}^n$ if and only if \[ \det(v_1 \mid v_2 \mid \cdots \mid v_n) \neq 0 . \]
Proof

See Example 6.25 for the argument with three vectors in $\mathbb{R}^3$; it works the same way for any $n$ vectors in $\mathbb{K}^n$. Let $A = (v_1 \mid v_2 \mid \cdots \mid v_n)$. If $\det A \neq 0$, then by Theorem 6.14 the vectors are linearly independent and $A$ is invertible, so every $w \in \mathbb{K}^n$ is $A \lambda$ for $\lambda = A^{-1} w$: a linear combination of $v_1, \dots, v_n$. So they span $\mathbb{K}^n$, and form a basis. Conversely, if they form a basis, they are linearly independent, and $\det A \neq 0$ by Theorem 6.14.□

We now want to show that the vector spaces we have been exploring have a basis. To do this we first introduce some terminology.

Definition 6.27 (Finite-dimensional vector space, Definition 6.21 in the notes) A vector space is called finite-dimensional if it can be spanned by a finite number of vectors.

So $\mathbb{R}^n$ over $\mathbb{R}$, $\mathbb{C}^n$ over $\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbb{C}^n$ over $\mathbb{C}$ are all finite-dimensional: Definition 6.18 and Exercise 6.19 give finite spanning sets.

We start by showing that a linearly independent set cannot contain more vectors than a basis of the same space.

Theorem 6.28 (Theorem 6.26 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$, let $B \subseteq V$ be a basis with $|B| = n \in \mathbb{N}$, and let $S \subseteq V$ be a linearly independent subset. Then $|S| \leq |B|$.

This generalises Corollary 6.11 from $\mathbb{K}^n$ to any vector space with a finite basis. In $\mathbb{K}^n$, with $B$ the standard basis $e_1, \dots, e_n$ (Definition 6.18 and Exercise 6.19), it says that a linearly independent set has at most $n$ elements, which is Corollary 6.11.

Proof

We argue by contradiction: suppose that $S$ has more vectors than $B$. Let $B = \{v_1, \dots, v_n\}$, and choose distinct $w_1, \dots, w_{n+1} \in S$. One at a time, we replace the vectors $v_i$ of the basis by the vectors $w_i$ until $w_{n+1}$ is a linear combination of $w_1, \dots, w_n$.

Since $B$ spans $V$, $w_1 = \lambda_1 v_1 + \cdots + \lambda_n v_n$ for some $\lambda_i \in \mathbb{K}$. Not all $\lambda_i$ are $0$, since $w_1 \neq \mathbf{0}$: a linearly independent set does not contain $\mathbf{0}$. Reordering the $v_i$, we may assume $\lambda_1 \neq 0$, and then

\[ v_1 = \frac{1}{\lambda_1} \bigl( w_1 - \lambda_2 v_2 - \cdots - \lambda_n v_n \bigr) \]

is a linear combination of $B^{(1)} := \{w_1, v_2, \dots, v_n\}$. So $B^{(1)}$ spans $V$. This is the first exchange.

Since $B^{(1)}$ spans $V$, next $w_2 = \lambda_1' w_1 + \lambda_2' v_2 + \cdots + \lambda_n' v_n$. If $\lambda_2', \dots, \lambda_n'$ were all $0$, then $w_2$ would be a multiple of $w_1$, contradicting that $S$ is linearly independent. So, reordering $v_2, \dots, v_n$, we may assume $\lambda_2' \neq 0$, and as before $B^{(2)} := \{w_1, w_2, v_3, \dots, v_n\}$ spans $V$.

Continuing in this way, after $n$ exchanges $B^{(n)} = \{w_1, \dots, w_n\}$ spans $V$. Hence $w_{n+1} \in \operatorname{span} B^{(n)}$ is a linear combination of $w_1, \dots, w_n$, so $w_1, \dots, w_{n+1}$ are linearly dependent, contradicting that $S$ is linearly independent.□

Because the proof exchanges the vectors of a basis for vectors of $S$ one at a time, the result is sometimes called the Steinitz–Mac Lane exchange lemma.

Example 6.29 (Example 6.23 in the notes) Consider \[ S = \left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \right\} , \] and let $V = \operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}(S)$. Here \[ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} - \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} = \mathbf{0} , \] so $S$ is linearly dependent. Let $v_1 = (1, 0)$. Since $(1, 0) = (1, 1) - (0, 1)$, the set $S \setminus \{v_1\}$ still spans $V$ (Lemma 6.15). And $S \setminus \{v_1\} = \{(0, 1), (1, 1)\}$ is linearly independent, since neither vector is a multiple of the other (Example 6.3). Hence we have found a basis of $V$. Any of the three vectors could have been chosen as $v_1$, with the other two left as a basis: in the picture after Lemma 6.15, all three would be green.
Example 6.30 (Example 6.24 in the notes) Consider the vectors of $\mathbb{R}^3$, over the field $\mathbb{R}$, \[ S = \left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \right\} , \] and let $V = \operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}(S)$. Write $v_1 = (1, 0, 0)$, $v_2 = (-2, 0, 0)$ and $v_3 = (1, 1, 0)$. Here \[ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \frac{1}{2} \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + 0 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \] so $S$ is linearly dependent. This equation can be solved for $v_1$ and for $v_2$, each as a linear combination of the others: \[ \begin{aligned} v_1 &= -\frac{1}{2} v_2 + 0 v_3 , \\ v_2 &= -2 v_1 + 0 v_3 . \end{aligned} \] But it cannot be solved for $v_3$, whose coefficient in it is $0$: at least using this equation, we cannot write $v_3$ as a linear combination of the others. Since $v_1 = -\frac{1}{2} v_2 + 0 v_3$, the set $S \setminus \{v_1\}$ still spans $V$: putting $-\frac{1}{2} (-2, 0, 0)$ in place of $v_1$ turns any linear combination of the three vectors into one of the other two, \[ \begin{aligned} &\lambda_1 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_3 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \\ &\qquad = \Bigl( \lambda_2 - \frac{\lambda_1}{2} \Bigr) \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_3 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} . \end{aligned} \] Note that \[ \begin{aligned} &\operatorname{span}\left\{ \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \right\} \\ &\qquad = \left\{ a \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + b \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} : a, b \in \mathbb{R} \right\} \\ &\qquad = \left\{ \Bigl( \lambda_2 - \frac{\lambda_1}{2} \Bigr) \begin{pmatrix} -2 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_3 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \right. \\ &\qquad \qquad \left. \vphantom{\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}} : \lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3 \in \mathbb{R} \right\} , \end{aligned} \] since $\lambda_2 - \frac{\lambda_1}{2}$ and $\lambda_3$ can be any real numbers $a$ and $b$ (take $\lambda_1 = 0$, $\lambda_2 = a$ and $\lambda_3 = b$). In the first of these two displays, the left-hand side is any vector of $\operatorname{span} S$, and the right-hand side writes it as a linear combination of $(-2, 0, 0)$ and $(1, 1, 0)$ alone, so $\operatorname{span} S \subseteq \operatorname{span}\{(-2, 0, 0), (1, 1, 0)\}$. The other inclusion holds because any linear combination of those two is also one of all three, with coefficient $0$ on $v_1$. Each span contains the other, so they are the same: $\operatorname{span}\{(-2, 0, 0), (1, 1, 0)\} = \operatorname{span} S$. The span of two of the vectors is the span of all three, because the third is a linear combination of them (Exercise 6.31 below). And $S \setminus \{v_1\} = \{(-2, 0, 0), (1, 1, 0)\}$ is linearly independent, since neither vector is a multiple of the other. Hence we have found a basis of $V$. In fact $v_3 = (1, 1, 0)$ is not a linear combination of the other vectors of $S$ at all, since $v_1$ and $v_2$ only span the $x_1$-axis, so it cannot be removed without making the span smaller; either of the other two can be removed to give a basis. In the picture below, $(1, 1, 0)$ turns red when you point at it.

The set $S$ of Example 6.30 in $\mathbb{R}^3$, with its span shaded: the plane $x_3 = 0$. Point at a vector to highlight it: green if it lies in the span of the others, so that removing it leaves the span as it is (Lemma 6.15), red if removing it makes the span smaller. Click it to remove it. Every arrow is drawn with length 1, so $(-2, 0, 0)$ looks as long as $(1, 0, 0)$, pointing the other way. Drag anywhere else to turn the picture.

Exercise 6.31 (a vector already in the span) Let $v_1, v_2, w \in \mathbb{R}^3$, and suppose that $w \in \operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2\}$. Show that \[ \operatorname{span}\{w, v_1, v_2\} = \operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2\} . \] Equivalently: if $w$ is a linear combination of $v_1$ and $v_2$, then $\operatorname{span}\{w\} \subseteq \operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2\}$ and $\operatorname{span}\{w, v_1, v_2\} = \operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2\}$.
Solution

Write $w = \alpha v_1 + \beta v_2$ with $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$. Every vector of $\operatorname{span}\{w\}$ is $\lambda w = (\lambda \alpha) v_1 + (\lambda \beta) v_2$, so $\operatorname{span}\{w\} \subseteq \operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2\}$.

Every vector of $\operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2\}$ is $\mu_1 v_1 + \mu_2 v_2 = 0 w + \mu_1 v_1 + \mu_2 v_2$, so it lies in $\operatorname{span}\{w, v_1, v_2\}$. Conversely, every vector of $\operatorname{span}\{w, v_1, v_2\}$ is

\[ \begin{aligned} \lambda w + \mu_1 v_1 + \mu_2 v_2 &= (\lambda \alpha + \mu_1) v_1 \\ &\quad + (\lambda \beta + \mu_2) v_2 , \end{aligned} \]

which lies in $\operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2\}$. So the two spans are equal. This is the argument in the proof of Lemma 6.15, with $S = \{w, v_1, v_2\}$ and $v = w$: a vector that lies in the span of the others can be added, or removed, without changing the span.□

Remark (What we learn in the next two theorems) In a finite-dimensional vector space,
  • any finite spanning set can be reduced to a basis (Theorem 6.32), and
  • any linearly independent set can be extended to a basis (Theorem 6.33).

You can see both in the picture after Lemma 6.15, a random set of vectors with its span shaded. With its Lemma 6.15 button, the vectors span the shaded space. Clicking a green vector removes it and leaves the span as it is. When every vector left is red, none of them is in the span of the others, so what is left is linearly independent and still spans the shaded space: it is a basis of that space, found inside the set you started with. That is Theorem 6.32. With its Theorem 6.33 button, you start from linearly independent vectors, and a click outside the shaded span adds a vector and keeps the set linearly independent. Once the span is the whole space, the set is a basis that contains the vectors you started with. That is Theorem 6.33.

Theorem 6.32 (Theorem 6.22 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$, and let $S \subseteq V$ be a finite set with $\operatorname{span} S = V$. Then $S$ contains a basis of $V$. In particular, every finite-dimensional vector space has a basis.
Proof

The idea of the proof is the same as in the picture after Lemma 6.15: if the set is not linearly independent, we can remove a vector without changing the span, one of the green ones, and we keep doing so until the set is linearly independent.

If $S$ is linearly independent, then $S$ is already a basis. If $S$ is linearly dependent, then by Lemma 6.15 there is a $v_1 \in S$ such that $S_1 = S \setminus \{v_1\}$ still spans $V$. If $S_1$ is linearly independent, it is a basis; if not, Lemma 6.15 again gives a smaller set $S_2$ that still spans $V$. Continuing, we get sets $S, S_1, S_2, \dots$ with $|S_{i+1}| = |S_i| - 1$, and since $S$ is finite this must stop: at some step $k$ the set $S_k$ is linearly independent and spans $V$, so it is a basis of $V$. For the last sentence, a finite-dimensional vector space has a finite spanning set $S$, by Definition 6.27.□

As well as taking a linearly dependent spanning set and removing vectors to get a basis, we can also start with a linearly independent set and add vectors to it until we have a basis.

Theorem 6.33 (Theorem 6.25 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$, and assume that $V$ is finite-dimensional. Then any linearly independent subset $S \subseteq V$ can be extended to a basis $B$ of $V$: there is a basis $B$ of $V$ with $S \subseteq B$.
Proof

Let $S = \{v_1, v_2, \dots, v_r\}$ and set

\[ V_r := \operatorname{span}\{v_1, v_2, \dots, v_r\} , \]

a subspace of $V$ with basis $v_1, \dots, v_r$. If $V_r = V$, we are done. Otherwise there is a vector $v_{r+1} \in V$ with $v_{r+1} \notin V_r$, so in particular $v_{r+1} \neq \mathbf{0}$. We claim that $v_1, \dots, v_r, v_{r+1}$ are linearly independent. Suppose that

\[ \lambda_1 v_1 + \cdots + \lambda_r v_r + \lambda_{r+1} v_{r+1} = \mathbf{0} . \]

If $\lambda_{r+1} \neq 0$, then

\[ \begin{aligned} v_{r+1} &= -\frac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_{r+1}} v_1 - \cdots - \frac{\lambda_r}{\lambda_{r+1}} v_r \\ &\in V_r , \end{aligned} \]

which contradicts $v_{r+1} \notin V_r$. Hence $\lambda_{r+1} = 0$, and then all the other $\lambda_i$ are $0$ too, since $v_1, \dots, v_r$ are linearly independent. So set $V_{r+1} := \operatorname{span}\{v_1, \dots, v_{r+1}\}$, again a subspace, with basis $v_1, \dots, v_{r+1}$, and proceed as before: either $V_{r+1} = V$, or we find a $v_{r+2}$, and so on. This gives a chain of subspaces

\[ V_r \subsetneq V_{r+1} \subsetneq \cdots \subseteq V \]

that strictly increase. Since $V$ is finite-dimensional, the process must stop, by Theorem 6.28: there is a finite $k$ with $V_k = V$, and then $v_1, \dots, v_k$ is a basis of $V$ that contains $S$.□

That the process must stop rests on Theorem 6.28 above, which is Theorem 6.26 in the notes: a linearly independent set has at most as many vectors as a basis. By Theorem 6.32, $V$ has a finite basis, so the chain cannot go on for ever. For $V = \mathbb{K}^n$ this is Corollary 6.11.

You can watch Theorem 6.33 at work in the picture after Lemma 6.15: press its Theorem 6.33 button, and add vectors until the span is the whole space.

Dimension of a vector space

We saw that every finite-dimensional vector space has a basis. Generally there are many bases for a vector space, but they have something in common: any two bases of a finite-dimensional vector space have the same size.

Definition 6.34 (Dimension, Definition 6.28 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space, and assume that $V$ has a basis $B$ with finitely many elements. Then the dimension of $V$ is $|B|$, written $\dim V$.

This makes sense because any two bases of $V$ have the same number of elements, which is Corollary 6.27 in the notes: if $A$ and $B$ are bases of $V$ and $A$ is finite, Theorem 6.28 with $S = B$ gives $|B| \leq |A|$, and then with the roles swapped, $|A| \leq |B|$. For example, $\dim \mathbb{R}^n = n$, by the standard basis (Definition 6.18), and $\dim \{\mathbf{0}\} = 0$ (Definition 6.20).

Remark (A warning)
Show me the warning, I'm ready

Note that the definition of dimension depends on the field we consider. For $V = \mathbb{C}^2$ over $\mathbb{C}$, we have the standard basis

\[ e_1 = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \quad e_2 = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} , \]

meaning $\dim \mathbb{C}^2 = 2$. Remember that, for $\mathbb{K}^n$ as a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$, $n$ vectors form a basis if and only if the determinant of the matrix with them as columns is non-zero (Lemma 6.26). So $\mathbb{C}^2$ has many other bases over $\mathbb{C}$, each with two vectors. For instance, each of the sets

\[ \begin{aligned} &\left\{ \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ i \end{pmatrix} \right\} , \\ &\left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ i \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} \right\} , \\ &\left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} , \begin{pmatrix} i \\ -i \end{pmatrix} \right\} \end{aligned} \]

is a basis of $\mathbb{C}^2$ over $\mathbb{C}$, since the determinants are non-zero:

\[ \begin{aligned} \begin{vmatrix} i & 0 \\ 0 & i \end{vmatrix} &= i \cdot i - 0 \cdot 0 = -1 , \\ \begin{vmatrix} 1 & i \\ i & 1 \end{vmatrix} &= 1 \cdot 1 - i \cdot i = 2 , \\ \begin{vmatrix} 1 & i \\ 1 & -i \end{vmatrix} &= 1 \cdot (-i) - i \cdot 1 = -2i . \end{aligned} \]

But $\mathbb{C}^2$ could also be viewed as a vector space over $\mathbb{R}$. Then $e_1, e_2$ are no longer a basis, since linear combinations of $e_1, e_2$ with real coefficients give us only vectors in $\mathbb{R}^2 \subseteq \mathbb{C}^2$. In this case the four vectors

\[ \begin{aligned} e_1 &= \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , & i e_1 &= \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \\ e_2 &= \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} , & i e_2 &= \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ i \end{pmatrix} \end{aligned} \]

do form a basis (Exercise 6.19), so as a vector space over $\mathbb{R}$ we have $\dim \mathbb{C}^2 = 4$.

This dependence on the field $\mathbb{K}$ is sometimes emphasised by putting $\mathbb{K}$ as a subscript, that is, $\dim_{\mathbb{K}} V$ is the dimension of $V$ over $\mathbb{K}$. In our example we found

\[ \dim_{\mathbb{C}} \mathbb{C}^2 = 2 \quad \text{and} \quad \dim_{\mathbb{R}} \mathbb{C}^2 = 4 . \]

The difference can be even more dramatic: viewing $\mathbb{R}$ as a vector space over $\mathbb{R}$ and over $\mathbb{Q}$ we get $\dim_{\mathbb{R}} \mathbb{R} = 1$ but $\dim_{\mathbb{Q}} \mathbb{R}$ is infinite (in fact, uncountable).

Exercise 6.35 (Lemma 6.29 in the notes) Let $S \subseteq V$ be linearly independent with $\operatorname{span} S \neq V$, and let $v \in V \setminus \operatorname{span} S$. Show that $S \cup \{v\}$ is linearly independent.
Solution

Take any $v_1, \dots, v_k \in S$, and suppose that $\lambda_1 v_1 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k + \lambda v = \mathbf{0}$. If $\lambda \neq 0$, then $v = -\frac{1}{\lambda} (\lambda_1 v_1 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k) \in \operatorname{span} S$, a contradiction. So $\lambda = 0$, and then $\lambda_1 = \cdots = \lambda_k = 0$, since $S$ is linearly independent. This is the step at the heart of the proof of Theorem 6.33.□

We now collect some useful facts about the dimension.

Corollary 6.36 (Corollary 6.30 in the notes) Let $V$ be a finite-dimensional vector space, and let $S \subseteq V$.
  1. If $S$ is linearly independent, then $S$ has at most $\dim V$ elements.
  2. If $S$ spans $V$, then $S$ has at least $\dim V$ elements.
  3. If $S$ is linearly independent and has $\dim V$ elements, then $S$ is a basis of $V$.
  4. If $S$ spans $V$ and has $\dim V$ elements, then $S$ is a basis of $V$.
Proof

$V$ has a finite basis by Theorem 6.32, so $\dim V$ is defined.

(i) This is Theorem 6.28 in the language of dimension.

(ii) If $S$ is infinite there is nothing to prove. If $S$ is finite, it contains a basis $B$ of $V$ by Theorem 6.32, and $|B| = \dim V$, so $|S| \geq \dim V$.

(iii) Suppose that $S$ is linearly independent with $\dim V$ elements, but not a basis. Then $S$ does not span $V$, and by Theorem 6.33 it extends to a basis $B$ with $S \subsetneq B$. Then $|B| > |S| = \dim V$, contradicting that every basis of $V$ has $\dim V$ elements.

(iv) By Theorem 6.32, $S$ contains a basis $B$ of $V$. Then $|B| = \dim V = |S|$, and since $B \subseteq S$, $B = S$.□

This gives a simpler test for a basis than the definition. If we know $\dim V$, then a set with $\dim V$ elements is a basis as soon as it is linearly independent, or as soon as it spans $V$: in the finite-dimensional case we only have to check one of the two conditions.

Finally we turn to subspaces, where two facts follow from the counting above.

Proposition 6.37 (Proposition 6.31 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$ with $\dim V = n$, and let $U$ be a subspace of $V$.
  1. $U$ is finite-dimensional, and $\dim U \leq \dim V$.
  2. If $\dim U = \dim V$, then $U = V$.
Proof

(i) Suppose, for a contradiction, that $U$ had more than $n$ linearly independent vectors. Then we could choose vectors of $U$ one at a time, each outside the span of those already chosen:

\[ \begin{aligned} & u_1 \in U \setminus \{\mathbf{0}\} , \\ & u_2 \in U \setminus \operatorname{span}\{u_1\} , \\ & \qquad \vdots \\ & u_{n+1} \in U \setminus \operatorname{span}\{u_1, \dots, u_n\} . \end{aligned} \]

By Exercise 6.35 each new vector keeps the set linearly independent, so $u_1, \dots, u_{n+1}$ are $n + 1$ linearly independent vectors of $V$. That contradicts Corollary 6.36(i), by which a linearly independent subset of $V$ has at most $\dim V = n$ elements. So $U$ has at most $n$ linearly independent vectors, which makes $U$ finite-dimensional with $\dim U \leq n = \dim V$.

(ii) Suppose $\dim U = \dim V = n$ but $U \neq V$. Take a basis $B_U = \{u_1, \dots, u_n\}$ of $U$. Since $U \neq V$, $B_U$ does not span $V$, so there is a $v \in V \setminus \operatorname{span} B_U$, and by Exercise 6.35 the set $B_U \cup \{v\}$ is linearly independent. That is $n + 1$ linearly independent vectors in $V$, which again contradicts Corollary 6.36(i).□

Complements and dimensions of direct sums

Chapter 5 put two subspaces together with the direct sum of Definition 5.22. Now that we can count, the question is what the dimension of the result is, and the answer is the one you would guess: dimensions add. The example to keep in mind is $\mathbb{R}^3$ cut into the $xy$-plane and the $z$-axis, which is Example 5.32 in the notes, where a space of dimension $3$ becomes the direct sum of spaces of dimensions $2$ and $1$.

Proposition 6.38 (Proposition 6.32 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$ and let $U, W \subseteq V$ be finite-dimensional subspaces with $U \cap W = \{\mathbf{0}\}$. Then \[ \dim (U \oplus W) = \dim U + \dim W . \]
Proof

Let $B_U$ and $B_W$ be bases of $U$ and of $W$. We show that $B_U \cup B_W$ is a basis of $U \oplus W$.

It spans. Every $v \in U \oplus W$ can be written as $v = u + w$ with $u \in U$ and $w \in W$. Then $u \in \operatorname{span} B_U$ and $w \in \operatorname{span} B_W$, so $v$ is a linear combination of $B_U \cup B_W$.

It is linearly independent. Suppose some linear combination of $B_U \cup B_W$ gives $\mathbf{0}$. Gathering the $B_U$ terms into $u$ and the $B_W$ terms into $w$ leaves $\mathbf{0} = u + w$ with $u \in U$ and $w \in W$. By Theorem 5.23 the decomposition of a vector of a direct sum is unique, and $\mathbf{0} = \mathbf{0} + \mathbf{0}$ is one such decomposition, so $u = \mathbf{0}$ and $w = \mathbf{0}$. As $B_U$ and $B_W$ are each linearly independent, every coefficient was $0$.

So $B_U \cup B_W$ is a basis of $U \oplus W$. The two bases share no vector: a shared one would lie in $U \cap W = \{\mathbf{0}\}$, and no basis contains $\mathbf{0}$. Hence $B_U \cap B_W = \varnothing$, so $|B_U \cup B_W| = |B_U| + |B_W|$, which is the statement about dimensions.□

Dropping the assumption $U \cap W = \{\mathbf{0}\}$ changes the count in the way you would expect: the overlap gets counted once instead of twice.

Proposition 6.39 (Proposition 6.33 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$ and let $U, W \subseteq V$ be finite-dimensional subspaces. Then \[ \begin{aligned} \dim (U + W) &= \dim U + \dim W \\ &\quad - \dim (U \cap W) . \end{aligned} \]
Proof (outline, as in the notes)

Take a basis $A$ of $U \cap W$, and extend it by Theorem 6.33 to a basis $B_U$ of $U$, and separately to a basis $B_W$ of $W$. Then $B_U \cup B_W$ is a basis of $U + W$, by the same argument as in Proposition 6.38, and by construction the two bases overlap in exactly $A$, so

\[ |B_U \cup B_W| = |B_U| + |B_W| - |A| . \]

Read as dimensions, that is the statement.□

Putting $U \cap W = \{\mathbf{0}\}$ gives $\dim (U \cap W) = 0$ and recovers Proposition 6.38, a direct sum being exactly a sum with trivial intersection.

Given a subspace $U$ of $V$, we may want to know how to build it up into the whole of $V$. To do that with no overlap is to ask for a direct sum, which gives the following name.

Definition 6.40 (Complement, Definition 6.34 in the notes) Let $V$ be a vector space over $\mathbb{K}$ and let $U$ be a subspace of $V$. A subspace $W$ of $V$ is called a complement of $U$ in $V$ if \[ V = U \oplus W . \]
Example 6.41 (Example 6.35 in the notes) Some examples of complements.
  1. Let $V = \mathbb{R}^2$ and $U = \operatorname{span}\{v\}$ for some $v \in V \setminus \{\mathbf{0}\}$, so that $U$ is a line through the origin. For any $v' \in V$ with $\{v, v'\}$ a basis of $\mathbb{R}^2$, \[ \mathbb{R}^2 = \operatorname{span}\{v\} \oplus \operatorname{span}\{v'\} , \] so every other line through the origin is a complement of $U$. A complement is therefore not unique.
  2. Let $U = \operatorname{span}\{(i, 1, i),\, (0, i, 1)\} \subseteq \mathbb{C}^3$. Then $W = \operatorname{span}\{e_1\}$ is a complement, since $e_1 \notin U$ and so $\{(i, 1, i),\, (0, i, 1),\, e_1\}$ is linearly independent, a linearly independent set of size $3$ in $\mathbb{C}^3$ being a basis by Corollary 6.36(iii). One way to see that the set is linearly independent is to take the three vectors as the columns of a matrix: \[ \det \begin{pmatrix} i & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & i & 0 \\ i & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix} = 2 \neq 0 , \] and Lemma 6.26 turns a non-zero determinant into a basis.
  3. Part (ii) generalises. Let $U \subseteq \mathbb{K}^n$ have basis $v_1, v_2, \dots, v_k$. To find a complement of $U$ we must find $v_{k+1}, \dots, v_n \in \mathbb{K}^n$ such that $v_1, \dots, v_k, v_{k+1}, \dots, v_n$ is a basis of $\mathbb{K}^n$. With that done, \[ W := \operatorname{span}\{v_{k+1}, \dots, v_n\} \] is a complement of $U$ in $\mathbb{K}^n$.

The last of those is a recipe, and Theorem 6.33 says it never gets stuck, so a complement always exists.

Proposition 6.42 (Proposition 6.36 in the notes) Let $V$ be a finite-dimensional vector space over $\mathbb{K}$ and let $U \subseteq V$ be a proper subspace. Then there is a subspace $W$ which is a complement of $U$ in $V$.
Proof

Let $B_U$ be a basis of $U$ and extend it, using Theorem 6.33, to a basis $B_V$ of $V$. Put $B_W := B_V \setminus B_U$ and $W := \operatorname{span} B_W$. We claim $W$ is a complement.

First, $U + W = V$, because $U + W$ contains every vector of $B_V$ and therefore contains $\operatorname{span} B_V = V$.

Second, $U \cap W = \{\mathbf{0}\}$. Let $v \in U \cap W$. Then $v$ is a linear combination of $B_U$, and also a linear combination of $B_W$. Subtracting the two expressions writes $\mathbf{0} = v - v$ as a linear combination of the vectors of $B_V$, and if $v \neq \mathbf{0}$ that combination has a non-zero coefficient, contradicting the linear independence of $B_V$. So $v = \mathbf{0}$.

Hence $V = U \oplus W$.□

More revision: a question on bases

From the same past MATH10015 paper as the earlier revision questions, with the numbers changed a little. That set needed only Chapters 1 to 4; this one needs Chapter 6, so it waits until here.

Exercise 6.43 (a basis, and a linearly independent set) Let \[ X = \left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} ,\; \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} \right\} \subseteq \mathbb{C}^3 , \] \[ Y = \left\{ \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2i \end{pmatrix} ,\; \begin{pmatrix} i \\ -2 \end{pmatrix} \right\} \subseteq \mathbb{C}^2 . \]
  1. Is $X$ a basis for $\mathbb{C}^3$ over $\mathbb{C}$? Justify your answer briefly.
  2. Is $Y$ a $\mathbb{C}$-linearly independent subset of $\mathbb{C}^2$? Justify your answer briefly.
Hint, if you would like one

Definition 6.16. A basis of $V$ has to do two things: span $V$, and be linearly independent. Failing either one is enough to answer (i), and the quicker of the two here is spanning: look at the third coordinate of a linear combination.

Definition 6.1. Vectors $v_1, \dots, v_k$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb{K}$ if the only $\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k \in \mathbb{K}$ with $\lambda_1 v_1 + \cdots + \lambda_k v_k = \mathbf{0}$ are $\lambda_1 = \cdots = \lambda_k = 0$. For two vectors that comes to asking whether one is a scalar multiple of the other, and the scalar may be complex.

Solution

(i) No. Every linear combination of the two vectors of $X$ is

\[ \lambda_1 \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} + \lambda_2 \begin{pmatrix} 3 \\ i \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} \lambda_1 + 3\lambda_2 \\ i \lambda_2 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix} , \]

whose third coordinate is $0$ whatever $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$ are. So $\begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 0 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix}$ is not in $\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}} X$, which means $X$ does not span $\mathbb{C}^3$ and part (a) of Definition 6.16 fails. $X$ is not a basis.

There is a second reason, and it does not need the vectors at all: $X$ has two elements while $\dim \mathbb{C}^3 = 3$, and by Corollary 6.36 a set that spans $V$ has at least $\dim V$ elements. Two vectors can never span $\mathbb{C}^3$.

(ii) No. The second vector is $i$ times the first:

\[ i \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 2i \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} i \\ 2i^2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} i \\ -2 \end{pmatrix} . \]

So, writing $y_1$ and $y_2$ for the two vectors, $i y_1 - y_2 = \mathbf{0}$ is a linear combination giving $\mathbf{0}$ whose coefficients $i$ and $-1$ are not both zero. By Definition 6.1, $Y$ is linearly dependent over $\mathbb{C}$, so it is not a linearly independent set.□

The scalar that does it is $i$, which is not real. Over $\mathbb{R}$ the same two vectors are independent, because no real multiple of $y_1$ is $y_2$. Whether a set is independent depends on the field, which is the point of Example 6.5.

B2 from the same paper

It is already here, as Exercise 4.27 in the earlier revision set, so it has not been printed twice. When it was set, two of its parts reached beyond the chapter it sits in: part (ii), that $S(A, b)$ is a subspace exactly when $b = \mathbf{0}$, and part (iv), about $S(B^k, \mathbf{0})$. You now have what they need. Part (ii) is the subspace test of Theorem 5.8, applied to a subset of $\mathbb{R}^3$, and the quickest way in is to ask whether $\mathbf{0}$ is in the set at all.
